## Page 4

Research Interests
Our research interests focus on the Green and Low-Carbon Resource Recycling and
Pollution Control of Critical Elements.
Green and low-carbon resource recycling and pollution control of critical elements in urban
industrial, mining solid wastes, and their derived wastewater and waste gases.
2. Developing new photochemical, electrochemical, and thermochemical conversion
processes, technologies, eco-materials, and equipment；
3. Atomic-scale mechanism characterization technology of bulk and interface transformation
during the circulation of key elements
4. Seawater desalination, resource utilzation, and COz capture
More details can be found by scan our group website：
https://faculty.sustech.edu.cn/chenh3

## Page 15

1. What is the definition of earth environment？
Earth's environment consists of five closely related and interacting spheres
（1） The Hydrosphere: Water
（2） The Atmosphere: Air
（3） The Geosphere: Rock, mineral matter, soil
（4） The Anthrosphere: Things humans make and do
（5） The Biosphere: Living organisms.

Energy and matter change intensively
in these five spheres of the earth system.

## Page 22

Some Important Biogeochemical Cycles
• Nitrogen cycle: Vast amount of N2 gas in the atmosphere, bound in N compounds by
microorganisms and industrial processes, essential plant nutrient, released back to the atmosphere
by denitrification
• Oxygen cycle: Vast reservoir in atmosphere, present in water and rocks, Oz released by
photosynthesis, taken up by respiration
• Sulfur cycle: In atmosphere as SOz and sulfate aerosols, in many minerals, sulfur compounds
acted upon by bacteria, in proteins in the biosphere
• Phosphorous cycle: Endogenous minor atmospheric component, essential in biomolecules such
as nucleic acids, plant nutrient crucial for food crops, excess in water can cause eutrophication, a
pollution condition in water

## Page 5

Rapid depletion of groundwater in some areas
Especially problematic in California's Central Valley-the vegetable garden of
the U.S.
Severe in many areas of Africa and Asia
Effects of groundwater shortages
Lowered water table, no more water can be pumped
Intrusions of saline water, especially in coastal areas
Irreversible damage to aquifer compaction, cannot be recharged
Surface subsidence, even structural damage
Depletion of surface water in lakes and rivers
Contributes to desertification-productive land becomes desert

## Page 15

2.6 Gases in Water
O for fish
COz to support algal growth
Henry's law: The solubility of a gas in a liquid is directly proportional to the partial pressure of the
gas in contact with the liquid.
Atmospheric chemists often define the Henry solubility as
= Ca/p.
（1）
Here, Ca is the concentration of a species in the aqueous
phase, and p is the partial pressure of that species in the gas
phase under equilibrium conditions.
The Sl unit for HP is mol m-3 Pa-. However, often the
unit M atm-l is used since Ca is usually expressed in M
（1M=1mol dm-3） and p in atm （1 atm =101 325 Pa）.
Sl: International System of Units （SI）

2.8 Alkalinity
Alkalinity is the capacity to accept H+ ion
Most commonly: HCOs + H* +> COz（ag）+ H20
Other common contributors: OH, COg2: CO2-+ H* HCOs；OH'+H* 4H20
Complete formula for alkalinity applicable over wide pH range
［alk］ = ［HCOs］+2［COs21+［OH］- ［H+］
Concentration of COg3 is multiplied by 2, because each COgcan neutralize 2 H+
Note : pH is an intensity factor, can be defined as basicity.
alkalinity is a capacity factor.

•Pollutants such as from metal plating wastes

## Page 2

Basics Reduction/Oxidation Concepts
Oxidation number
The oxidation number is expressed by putting a Roman number representing the
oxidation number in parenthesis after the symbol of an element in the molecular formula.
Au（l）CI
Au （III）Cls
Oxidation: An increase in the oxidation number
Reduction: An decrease in the oxidation number.
Redox reactions involve changes in oxidation states of elements.

## Page 3

Basics Reduction/Oxidation Concepts
Oxidizing agent: A reagent which can increase the oxidation number of an element in a
given substance. These reagents are called as oxidants.
Reducing agent: A reagent which lowers the oxidation number of an element in a given
substance. These reagents are also called as reductants.
Reduction：
Reverse of oxidation
e.g.， 2 CuO（s）-•2 Cu（s） +0z （g）
Oxidation：
Reaction of an element or a compound with Oz to give an oxide
e.g, 4 Na（s） + 0z（9）—•2 NazO（s） （sodium oxide）
2H2（g） + 0z（g） —、2H20 （I） （hydrogen oxide）

Basics Reduction/Oxidation Concepts
Identify a Redox reaction
Redox reactions are most easily visualized as a transfer of electrons.
In Redox reaction, electrons are transferred from one species to another in different ways.
Directly as clectrons. I. e. Fe2t + Cu2 -Fe't + Cut
As Hydrogen atoms. The hydrogen atom contains a proton, H' and an electron, e.
AH2
• A+2e+2H
Asa Hydride ion :H. The hydride ion has two electrons and is highly reactive. In
biological systems it is directly transferred to NAD-linked dehydrogenases.
Through the direct combination with oxygen. Molecular oxygen combines with organic
reactants to oxidize hydrocarbons to alcohols, aldehydes to acids ect. I.e.
R—CHs+ 1/20一R—CH-OH

Basics Reduction/Oxidation Concepts
Half-reactions in Redox reaction
The reaction
Fe+ Cdz+ Fez++ Cd
can be obtained by adding the two following half-reactions：
Oxidation: Fe — Fez++ 2e-
Reduction: Cdz+ + 2e Cd
Overall: Fe + Cdz+ Fez+ + Cd

• Fe is oxidized by losing 2 electrons to yield Fe2+
•Cdzt ion is reduced by gaining 2e to give elemental Cd

## Page 10

The activity of the H* ion is used to express the extent to which water is acidic or
basic；
The activity of the electron, e-， is used to express the degree to which an aquatic
medium is oxidizing or reducing.
Water with a high hydrogen-ion activity, such as runoff from “acid rain”
，is acidic.
Water with a high electron activity, such as that in the anoxic digester of a sewage
treatment plant, is said to be reducing.
10

## Page 11

Water with a low Ht ion activity （high concentration of OH ）-such as landfill leachate
contaminated with waste sodium hydroxide—is basic.
Water with a low electron activity—highly chlorinated water, for example—is said to be
oxidizing.
Neither free electrons nor free H* ions as such are found dissolved in aquatic solution；
they are always strongly associated with solvent or solute species

3.3 Electron Activity and pE
The negative log of electron activity, pE expresses this activity over many orders of

## Page 28

4.8 Solute Exchange with Bottom Sediments
Important characteristics of bottom sediments: their ability to exchange cations with the
surrounding aquatic medium.
Cation exchange capacity （CEC） measures the capacity of a sediment to sorb cations.
A common method
1） treating the sediment with a solution of an ammonium salt so that all exchangeable
sites are occupied by NHat ion；
2） displacing the ammonium ion with a solution of NaCl；
3） determining the quantity of displaced ammonium ion.
The CEC values may then be expressed as the number of milliequivalents of ammonium
ion exchanged per 100g of dried sample.
28
SUSTechs

## Page 29

4.8 Solute Exchange with Bottom Sediments
Exchangeable cation status （ECS， 可交换阳离子程度） refers to the amounts of specific
ions bonded to a given amount of sediment.
•Common cations in sediments are H*， K*， NHa*， Cazt, Mg2t, Fezt, Mn2t, Zn2t, Cu2t, Nizt.
A common method
The determination of ECS basically consists of stripping all of the exchangeable metal
cations from the sediment sample with ammonium acetate.
Then, test the concentration of a specific ion.
CEC and ECS are expressed as milliequivalents per 100g of solid.
29
SUSTech

## Page 30

4.8.1 Trace-Level Metals in Suspended Matter and Sediments
Trace metals held in sediments and colloidal suspensions include cadmium, chromium，
cobalt, copper, manganese, molybdenum, and nickel
The availability of metals held in suspended particles, less available than those in
solution, but more than those in sediments.
Impact factors of availability：
the identity of the metal
chemical form （type of binding, oxidation state）
the nature of the suspended material
the type of organism taking up the metal
the physical and chemical conditions in the water.

4.8.2 Phosphorus Exchange with Bottom Sediments
Phosphorus is one of the key elements in aquatic chemistry and is the limiting nutrient in
the growth of algae and eutrophication under many conditions.
Forms of phosphorus in sediments
Phosphate minerals, CagOH（PO4）3
Nonoccluded phosphorus（非封闭性磷），such as PO 3- held on mineral surfaces
Occluded phosphorus（封闭性磷），with orthophosphate ions contained within mineral
matrix, such as in aluminosilicates
Organic phosphorus, in biomass （usually algal or bacterial）

## Page 33

Organic Compounds on Sediments and Suspended Matter
1. Sediments as sinks and repositories of organic matter.
2. Colloids may affect the transport of organic matter.
3. Sorption affects degradation of organic matter, sorbed organic matter undergoes
chemical degradation and biodegradation, at different rates and by different pathways
compared to organic matter in solution.
4. Common types of sediments considered for their organic binding abilities are Clays，
humic substances and complexes between clays and humic substances
5. Sorption generally inversely proportional to their water solubility

• Products of decomposition and mineralization of planktonic biomass

• Gases in interstitial water

5.2 Algae
Algae（藻类）are generally microscopic organisms that subsist on inorganic nutrients using
solar energy and producing biomass from carbon dioxide by photosynthesis.
Phycology（藻类学）is the study of algae.
7
SUSTedhE

## Page 8

5.2 Algae
Four major kinds of unicellular algae are of importance in environmental
chemistry
Chrysophyta（金藻门），are found in both freshwater and marine systems. They store
food as carbohydrate or oil；
Chlorophyta（绿藻门），green algae are responsible for most productivity in water；
Pyrrophyta（甲藻门），are motile with structures that enable them to move in water, thus
give them characteristics of protozoa；
Euglenophyta（裸藻门），exhibit characteristics of both plants and animals. Capable of
photosynthesis, these algae are not exclusively photoautotrophic, and they utilize
sources
biomass from other sources for at least part of their carbon needs.
8

•Carbon from COz or HCOs
• Nitrogen, generally from NOs
• Phosphorus from HPOa or HPO？
• Sulfur from SO？
• Trace elements （iron may be limiting）
Lichen（地衣）are algae and fungi in a symbiotic（共生）relationship
• Lichen involved in rock weathering process

5.3 Fungi（真菌）
Nonphotosynthetic（非光合作用），oxic eukaryotic（真核生物）organisms
• Wide range of morphology, often filamentous（细丝状）
• Generally do not grow well in water, but generate decomposition products that
get into water；
• Most important function in environment is breakdown of plant cellulose with
cellulase enzyme（纤维素酶）；
• Key role in producing humic substance from plant matter.

Morphology • Mobility • Presence of chloroplasts • Shells • Spore formation
Some protozoa have chloroplasts（叶绿素）and are photosynthetic

15.4 Protozoa
Protozoa（原生动物）are significant in water for several reasons
• Cause of some devastating（极度危险的）human and animal diseases （malaria，疟疾
sleeping sickness 嗜睡症，dysentery 痢疾）.
• Form mineral deposits （limestone）， formed by the deposition of shells from the
foramifera（有孔虫）group of protozoa
• Oxidation degrade biomass, especially in sewage treatment
• May affect bacteria active in degrading biodegradable substances by “grazing”（吞噬）
on bacterial cells.

## Page 35

Acid Mine Water
Mineral pyrite, FeSz, exposed by coal mining is oxidized largely by bacterial action.
2FeSz（s） + 2H20+ 702 4Ht+ 4SO. + 2Fe2+
4Fe2t + 02 + 4H* -4Fe3* +2H20
Several kinds of bacteria may be involved
• Thiobacillus ferrooxidans（氧化亚铁硫杆菌） • Metallogenium（生金菌属）
• Thiobacillus thiooxidans（氟化硫硫杆菌）•Ferrobacillus ferrooxidans（氟化亚铁杆菌）
Because of production of acid, bacteria involved must be very acid-tolerant.
An adverse effect of acid mine water is precipitation of amorphous, semigelatinous（半胶体）
hydrated iron（III） oxide.
• Fe3* + 3H,0 -Fe（OH），（S）+3H+

## Page 3

6.1 Nature and Types of Water Pollutants
Markers of water pollution（水污染的标记物） that show presence of pollution sources
• Herbicides（除草剂）indicate agricultural runoff
• Fecal coliform bacteria（类大肠杆菌） indicate sewage sources
• Pharmaceutical metabolites in domestic wastewater
Biomarkers of water pollution（水污染的生物标志物） are organisms that indicate pollution
• May accumulate pollutants
• May show effects from pollutant exposure
• Fish lipid tissue accumulates persistent organic pollutants
•Osprey（鱼鹰）at top of aquatic food web is a good biomarker

## Page 18

6.9 Oxygen, Oxidants, and Reductants
Biochemical oxygen demand（BOD， 生化需氧量）， refers to amount of oxygen consumed in a
volume of water by the biodegradable organic matter in it.
This parameter is commonly measured by determining the quantity of oxygen utilized by
suitable aquatic microorganisms during a 5-day period.
Chemical oxygen demand （COD，化学需氧量） is an indicative measure of the amount of
oxygen that can be consumed by reactions in a measured solution.

pollutants, especially those that have an affinity for lipid tissue and that resist
biodegradation.

For a specific substance,BCFs uostance concentration in organism
Substance concentration in water
Bioaccumulation factor（BAF，生物积累系数），defined in the same way, except that it
assumes that both the organism and its food are exposed similarly to a pollutant over a
long period of time.

6.11 Pesticides（农药） in Water
Numerous kinds of compounds added to soil and plants

•Fungicides（杀真菌剂）•Bactericides（杀菌剂）•Slimicides（杀黏菌剂）•Avicides（杀鸟剂）•
Piscicides（杀鱼剂）• Plant growth regulators• Plant defoliants（脱叶剂）• Plant desiccants
（脱水剂）
Insecticides（杀虫剂） and fungicides most important for human exposure because of
application near harvest time.
Herbicides（除草剂）most important water pollutants because of widespread application
directly onto soil.

## Page 32

Pesticides and Other Chemicals of Particular Concern for
Water Pollution
Several classes of pesticides and other chemicals are of particular concern as water
pollutants because of their potential effects. These are
1. Highly biodegradation resistant compounds
2. Known or probable carcinogens （致癌物）
3. Substances with adverse reproductive or developmental effects
4. Neurotoxins including cholinesterase（胆碱脂酶） inhibitors（抑制剂）
5. Acutely toxic substances
6. Known groundwater contaminants

Herbicides 除草剂
Herbicides applied to millions of acres of cropland worldwide to control weeds.
Herbicides commonly occur in surface water and groundwater, especially in intensely

used for weed control on corn and soybeans
• Although widely used to control weeds on crops genetically engineered to resist it，
glyphosate 草甘磷 has a very strong affinity for soil and rarely gets into water

condenses

Water condenses below dew point
• Condensation nuclei
Clouds are composed of microdroplets of water
•Coalesce to form larger droplets and precipitation

8.7 Global Climate and Microclimate
Climate
• Characteristic of a particular region
• Varies with season
Example: Alternating monsoons and dry seasons
• Ice age manifested by long-term change in climate
Humans may be modifying climate largely by pumping carbon dioxide into the
atmosphere causing warming
Microclimate
•Highly localized climate
Example: At soil surface shaded by plants
Effects of urbanization on microclimate
• Heat dome over cities
• City atmosphere up to 5°C warmer
• Counteracting cooling effect from particulate matter

## Page 37

Evolution of the Atmosphere
Approximately 3.5 billion years ago, the atmosphere was probably free of oxygen and
consisted of a variety of gases, such as carbon dioxide, water vapor, and perhaps even
methane, ammonia, and hydrogen.
Atmospheric oxygen from photosynthesis
•CO2 + H2O+ IN ｛CH2O｝+02
Evidence from iron oxide deposits
4Fe2t+02+ H20 2Fe20s+8H*

## Page 38

Evolution of the Atmosphere
The atmosphere was bombarded by intense, bond-breaking ultraviolet light, which
along with lightning and radiation from radionuclides, provided the energy to bring
about chemical reactions that resulted in the production of relatively complicated
molecules, including even amino acids and sugars.
From the rich chemical mixture in the sea, life molecules evolved.

## Page 2

9.1 Particles in the Atmosphere
Pollutant particles in the 0.001-10 wm range are commonly suspended in the air near
sources of pollution such as the urban atmosphere, industrial plants, highways, and
power plants.
Particles may be
•Inorganic•Organic • Biological （pollen, microorganisms）
Primary particles emitted directly to the atmosphere
• Desert sand•Sea salt • Pollen
2

## Page 3

9.1 Particles in the Atmosphere
Secondary particles formed from gases in the atmosphere
• H2SO4
• Ammonium salts
• Photochemical smog
Particles undergo aging
• Gas exchange with air
• Water loss or uptake
•Chemical reactions within particles
3

## Page 4

Terms Pertaining to Particles
Aerosol（气溶胶）：Colloidal-sized atmospheric particles, smaller that 100 micrometer
Condensation aerosol（凝结气溶胶）：Formed from gas or vapors
Dispersion aerosol（分散气溶胶）：Formed from grinding bulk solids or dispersion of
liquids
Fog（雾）：High level of water droplets
Haze（灰霾）：Decreased visibility due to particles
Mists（薄雾）：Liquid particles
Smoke（烟）：From incomplete combustion of carbonaceous fuel

## Page 7

9.3 Physical Processes for Particle Formation
Dispersion aerosols（分散气溶胶）
such as dusts, formed from the disintegration of larger particles.
•Coal grinding, formation of spray in cooling towers, blowing dirt from dry soil
• Generally larger, above 1 wm
• Less respirable and less harmful than condensation aerosols
Natural sources include
• Sea spray
• Windblown dust
• Volcanic dust
10

## Page 8

9.3 Physical Processes for Particle Formation
Sources from human activities include
• RoCk quarries
• Disturbed soil
• Dust from crop harvesting
Larger particles are less respirable in that they do not penetrate so far into the lungs as
smaller ones.
11

## Page 9

9.4 Chemical Processes for Particle Formation
Chemical processes in the atmosphere convert large quantities of atmospheric gases to
particulate matter.
Organic pollutants and nitrogen oxides causes formation of ozone and photochemical
smog.
Particles from chemical processes
• Generally smaller, below 1 wm
• Higher organic contents
• Higher contents of toxic substances
• Toxic elements （arsenic）
• Carcinogenic organics

Elevated levels of particles in the 1952 London air pollution episode
• 4000 more deaths than usual over 5 days

## Page 22

Control of Nitrogen Oxides
1. Prevention of production
•Low excess air firing to limit O in Nz + Oz—2NO
• Low combustion temperatures prevent NO formation
2. Removal from exhaust gas
•Sorption by base relatively ineffective due to low acidity of N oxides
• Most commonly by catalytic reduction with reducing agents
• Automotive exhaust catalysts reduce NOx with slight excess CO and hydrocarbons
Biofilters consisting of microorganisms on fixed or fluidized supports are an experimental
approach to NOx control

## Page 2

11 Organic Air Pollutants
•
11.1 Organic Compounds in the Atmosphere
Organic substances from the atmosphere can be lost by different routes, including：
（1） wet deposition, dissolution in precipitation （rainwater）；
（2） dry deposition；
（3） photochemical reactions；
（4） formation of and incorporation into particulate matter；
（5） uptake by plants, through the lipophilic cuticle（角质层） layer
Overall, reactions of organic atmospheric contaminants are particularly important in
determining their manner and rates of loss from the atmosphere

## Page 4

Global Distillation and Fractionation of Persistent Organic Pollutants（POP）
Organohalides carried to cold
polar regions where they condense
Organohalides carried to
cold mountainous regions
where they condense
Atmospheric heating in
equatorial and temperate
regions vaporizes organo-
halidecompounds
（E） The least volatile POP are deposited near their sources, those of relatively high
volatility are distilled into polar regions, and those of intermediate volatility are
deposited predominantly at mid-latitudes.

## Page 5

11.2 Biogenic Organic Compounds in the Atmosphere
Biogenic organic compounds（生物源有机化合物）in the atmosphere are those produced by
organisms. Biogenic compounds are abundant in the atmosphere of forest regions and are
significant participants in the atmospheric chemistry of these regions.
• Methane from bacteria is the most abundant organic in the atmosphere.
•Anoxic bacteria:2｛CH20｝ -CHA+COz
Flatulent emissions（泄气排放） from domesticated animals, add about 85 million metric tons
of methane to the atmosphere.
SUSTech

## Page 6

11.2 Biogenic Organic Compounds in the Atmosphere
Terpenes（萜烯）from vegetation, primarily pine（松树）and citrus trees（柑橘树），are second to
methane as organics in the atmosphere
• Generally very reactive with HO.， due to the double bond involved （active participant in
photochemical smog formation）.
• Form much of the small particulate matter in atmosphere, secondary organic aerosol
（SOA）.

# Chapter 12-13 2026.pdf

## Page 1

Chapter 12. Photochemical Smog
Originally， “smog" referred to reducing, sulfurous smog （SOz）
• Generated from coal smoke and fog in London
This Chapter deals with oxidizing photochemical smog characterized by
• Eye irritation
• Low visibility at low humidity
• Presence of oxidants including O3
Photochemical smog forms in the troposphere’s（对流层）planetary boundary layer（行星边界
Three key elements for photochemical smog formation：
1） UV radiation
2） Hydrocarbons
3） NOX
1

• Water-soluble products （acid, aldehydes醛）
• Inorganic byproducts, such as Os and HNOs

## Page 38

Types of Damage from Photochemical Smog
Damage to materials
• Effects of ozone and other oxidants on materials, especially cracking and
deterioration of the rubber
• Corrosion, especially from HSO4 and HNOs produced by oxidation of acid gases
Photochemical smog may be observed even in lightly populated rural areas
• Burning of savanna grasses in rural areas produces NOx and reactive hydrocarbons
that produce smog

Human activities strongly influence the atmosphere particularly through emissions that
1. Warm the atmosphere by absorbing outgoing infrared
2. Pollutant particles that scatter and reflect sunlight
3. Photochemically reactive species such as NOz

## Page 51

Methane and Other Greenhouse Gases
Greenhouse gases other than COz contribute to global warming
•Halocarbons•Hydrohalocarbons •N,0
Methane, CHa, is especially important
• Now at 1.8 ppm• Increasing about 0.02 ppm/year
Methane from several important sources
• Natural gas leakage•Coal mine emissions
•Petroleum recovery•Burning savannas, tropical forests
• From methane entrapped in thawing permafrost
Radiative forcing of methane
•Absorption of infrared per molecule
• About 25 times that of COz

• Electricity from renewable and nuclear sources
• Hydrogen fuel
• More efficient use of carbon-based fuels
• Carbon sequestration

## Page 55

Harmful Effects of Acid Precipitation
•Direct phytotoxicity to plants from strong acids
• Phytotoxicity（植物毒性）from acid-forming gases （SOz, NOx）
• Acidification of water bodies
• Acidification of soil
• Indirect phytotoxicity, especially from AI3* in soil
• Destruction of sensitive forests
• Respiratory effects on humans and other animals
•Corrosion of metals, such as in electrical relays
• Deterioration of stone

•03+ m 02+0（2 <325 nm）
and by a series of reactions the net result of which is
•03+0-02+02
About 350,000 metric tons of ozone are formed and destroyed in the stratosphere daily

## Page 58

Shielding Effect of the Ozone Layer
Ozone absorbs ultraviolet radiation very strongly in the range 220-330 nm
Effectively absorbs very damaging UV-B radiation in the range 290-320 nm
• Essential protection for life on Earth's surface
Partial destruction of the stratospheric ozone layer could have adverse effects
• Increased skin cancer in humans
• Increased incidence of cataracts
•Damage to food crop plants
•Lowered productivity of marine phytoplankton
58

## Page 59

Ozone Layer Destruction
1974 discovery of chlorofluorocarbon destruction of Os.
• Nobel Prize to Molina, Rowland, and Crutzen
Stratospheric reactions of chlorofluorocarbons
•CF.Cl+ I-Cle+ CF.Cl
Followed by catalytic destruction of ozone by Cl
• Cl+ 0s-CIO•+ 02
•CIO•+ O- Cl. + Oz（Clo to destroy more ozone）
•0g +0-0 + Oz（Net reaction up to 10,000 times per Clo）
An important reaction sequence involves formation and subsequent destruction of the
dimer from CIO• to give Cl that catalyzes Os destruction.
59

## Page 60

Antarctic ozone hole discovered in 1985
• Active forms of Cl are stored in （largely crystalline ice） stratospheric Antarctic clouds
during winter.
CIONO •Cl•HOCI
• Reaction of CIONOz with HCl produces photochemically reactive Clz.
CIONOz +HCI - CI +HNOs
• Starting in September ultraviolet solar radiation reaches these clouds producing active
Cl that catalyzes ozone destruction.
Cl+m-Clo+Cl。
HOCI + I HO• + Cl•

## Page 61

Green Chemistry Solutions to Stratospheric Ozone Depletion
Restrictions from 1986 Montreal Protocol on Substances that Deplete the Ozone Layer
•Ban on production of chlorofluorocarbons （CFCs）
• Now having noticeable effects on levels of ozone-depleting substances
Replacement of CFCs
• H-Containing compounds destroyed in the troposphere by HO• radical
• HCFCs such as HCFC-22, CHCIF2（still contain CI）
•HFCs such as HFC-134a, CH,FCFs （no Cl, but greenhouse gas）
• As of 2015, R-410A, a mixture of difluoromethane （CHzF2, called R-32） and
pentafluoroethane （CHFzCF; called R-125） had become popular
• Volatile hydrocarbons （popular in Europe but not allowed in U.S. because of
flammability）
•Carbon dioxide substitute for CFCs to make plastic foams
•Insulation•Food containers• Packing
61

• Melting glaciers
•Decreased agricultural productivity
• Adverse health effects

## Page 66

13.12 Nuclear Winter
Nuclear winter refers to catastrophic climate change that could follow full-scale nuclear
attack
• Huge amounts of sooty particulate matter carried to stratospheric altitudes
• Possible dramatic cooling effects
• Reflection back to space of solar radiation
• Less re-absorption of outgoing infrared radiation
• Less water vapor in the atmosphere to re-absorb outgoing infrared radiation
Closest thing to nuclear winter experienced by humankind has come from huge volcanic
eruptions
• Massive Tambora, Indonesia, volcanic eruption in 1815
• Followed in 1816 by “year without a summer"
• Enormous eruption of Yellowstone volcano would destroy civilization

## Page 15

Geochemistry
Geochemistry: deals with chemical species, reactions, and processes in the
lithosphere and their interactions with the other environmental spheres
Environmental geochemistry is the branch of geochemistry that explores the
complex interactions among the rock/water/air/life systems that determine the
chemical characteristics of the surface environment
16

## Page 16

Weathering of Rock in the Geosphere
Weathering: breakdown of rock to finely divided form or material in solution by physical
and chemical processes.
•Physical weathering involves physical processes such as erosion and
freezing/thawing.
•Chemical weathering involves geochemical processes.
•Biogeochemical weathering involves biogeochemical process.
17

## Page 17

Chemical Weathering
As a chemical phenomenon, weathering can be viewed as the result of the tendency of
the rock/water/mineral system to attain equilibrium.
• Extremely slowly in dry air, much faster in presence of water
• Water may be involved as a chemical reactant

rock.

## Page 25

Fertilizer Pollution
Runoff from crop fertilizer causes excessive algal growth in receiving waters
resulting in eutrophication.
Dead zone where Mississippi River enters Gulf of Mexico
• Reaches approximately 8000 square miles area
• Lighter fresh water layer blankets saline water
• Laden with algal nutrients from agricultural fertilizers upstream
• Excessive phytoplankton growth produces biomass that decays
• Consumes oxygen causing eutrophied water
• Hydrogen sulfide produced
25

15.9 Pesticides（农药） and their Residues in Soil
Four major categories of concern
1. Carryover to later growing season
2. Biological effects on organisms in terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems
• Bioaccumulation and transfer through food chains
3. Groundwater contamination
4. Effects on soil fertility
Herbicides are the most important soil contaminants.
26

15.10 Wastes and Pollutants in Soil
Soil receives wastes from the other environmental spheres including those deliberately
applied from the anthrosphere.
Black carbon is an important repository of organic wastes
• May be left over from burning crop residues
Humic substances in soil tend to bind organic wastes
Pollutants degrade in soil
• Primarily biodegradation.
•Rhizosphere（根际）where plant roots are located is a particularly active area for
biodegradation.
• Some chemical and photochemical degradation.

15.11 Soil Loss and Degradation
Soil subject to degradation processes that make it unsuitable for plants growth.
Soil erosion
• Water erosion has carried away millions of tons of topsoil
• Wind erosion
• Associated with formation of desert areas

15.11 Soil Loss and Degradation
Desertification
• Drought•Fertility loss• Vegetation loss
• Factors in desertification include
• Erosion•Climate variations• Water availability
• Loss of soil humus•Deterioration of chemical properties
Deforestation: Loss of forests
• Particular problem with tropical rain forests
• Major contributor to global warming

## Page 31

15.12 Saving the Land
Preventing soil erosion and deterioration is commonly called soil conservation.
Soil is conserved by several important practices.
• Terracing and contour plowing.
• Planting cover crops.
• Conservation tillage in which crops are planted in residue from the preceding
season.
Perennial（多年生）plants conserve soil.
• Grass for pasture and hay
• Trees
•A goal of genetic engineering in agriculture is development of perennial plants that
produce cereal grains.

## Page 34

Soil Restoration
Soil can be impaired by loss of fertility, erosion, buildup of salinity, contamination by
phytotoxins.
Active measures of soil restoration, also called restoration ecology, may be required to
restore productivity.
• Physical alteration of soil to add terraces and relatively flat areas not subject erosion
• Restoration of organic matter by planting high-biomass crops that are cultivated into
the soil
• Nutrients added
• Contaminants treated
• Excess acid or base neutralized
• Salts leached from soil
• Removal of toxic substances by phytoremediation in which pollutants are pulled from
soil by plant roots
36

## Page 1

Chapter 16 INDUSTRIAL ECOLOGY AND GREEN CHEMISTRY
16.1 Changing the Bad Old Ways
The old attitude: From American Chemical Industry—A History, by W. Haynes, Van
Nostrand Publishers, 1954：
“By sensible definition, any by-product of a chemical operation for which there is no
profitable use is a waste. The most convenient, least expensive way of disposing of said
waste—up the chimney or down the river—is the best."
SUSTechS

## Page 2

This attitude caused many pollution problems in all environmental spheres
• Polluted water
• Polluted air
• Adverse biological effects
• Unsafe industrial conditions
• Damaged geosphere
• Hazardous waste dumps
2
SUSTech

## Page 3

Dealing with Pollution and Wastes
Legislation（立法）and regulations（规定）implemented around 1970 to deal with
pollution problems
• Largely a command-and-control approach
• Heavy reliance on “end-of-pipe" measures
The regulatory approach has been effective
• But cumbersome and expensive
A more effective self-regulating approach needed
•Industrial Ecology
• Green Chemistry
3
SUSTech n

## Page 4

16.2 Green Chemistry
Green Chemistry is the sustainable practice of chemical science and technology
within the framework of the practice of industrial ecology in a manner that is safe and
non-polluting and that consumes minimum amounts of materials and energy while
producing little or no waste material and which minimizes the use and handling of
hazardous substances and does not release such substances to the environment.
4

16.2 Green Chemistry
Reaction
conditions，
catalysts
Recycle
Product
controlQg
Renewable
feedstocks
No waste
Degradability
Ilustration of the definition of green chemistry, which emphasizes renewable feedstocks，
exacting control, mild reaction conditions, maximum recycling of materials, minimal wastes，
and degradability of products that might enter the environment.
5

## Page 6

Twelve Principles of Green Chemistry
1. Prevent wastes
2. Maximum safety of chemicals and products
3. Minimize hazards in chemical synthesis
4. Use renewable feedstocks where possible
5. Use catalysts for optimum conditions of chemical synthesis and minimum byproducts
6. Avoid chemical derivatives such as blocking agents
6
SUSTechE

## Page 7

Twelve Principles of Green Chemistry
7. Maximize atom economy
8. Use safer reaction media
9. Maximize energy efficiency
10. Design for degradability of chemicals and products
11. Use in-process real-time monitoring and control to reduce wastes and pollution，
maximize safety and minimize energy consumption
12. Minimize the potential for accidents with processes that use materials unlikely to cause
explosions, fires, and harmful releases
7
a SUSTech

## Page 8

Green Chemistry is Sustainable Chemistry with respect to：
• Economics: Capability of being less costly, especially when environmental costs are
factored in
• Materials：
Use less material overall
Use recycled and renewable materials
Minimize use of virgin raw materials
• Waste：
Reduces and, ideally, eliminates wastes

## Page 14

Hazardous Substances to Avoid in the Practice of Green Chemistry
1. Combustible and flammable substances that burn readily or violently in the presence
of 02
2. Oxidizers that provide oxygen for combustion
3. Reactive substances likely to undergo rapid, violent, often unpredictable reactions
4. Corrosive substances, generally sources of H* or OH- that react destructively with
materials, particularly metals
5. Toxic substances that harm organisms; can often be predicted by structure-activity
relationships
• Heavy metals
• Persistent non-biodegradable organic materials
• Volatile organic compounds

16.11 Industrial Ecology
Industrial ecology is a comprehensive approach to production, distribution, utilization，
and termination of goods and services in a manner that
• Minimum inputs of raw materials and energy
• Minimum production of wastes
• Maximum circulation of materials within industrial systems.

Classification of Hazardous Waste
Listed Wastes Designated by EPA （about 450） Under Resource Conservation and
Recovery Act （RCRA）
Listed wastes denoted by a letter and number
F-type wastes from nonspecific sources（非特异性来源废物）
• Example： （F012） Quenching wastewater from metal heat-treating using cyanides
K-type wastes from specific sources（特定来源废物）
• Example：（K019） Heavy ends from ethylene dichloride distillation
P-type acute hazardous wastes（急性危险废物）from nonspecific sources capable of
causing fatalities or serious illness
• Example： （P056） Fluorine
U-type miscellaneous hazardous wastes（杂项危险废物）
• Example：（U032 Calcium chromate）

## Page 11

19.4 Flammable and Combustible Substances
• Flash point（闪点）is the temperature of ignition in proximity to a flame under specified
conditions
Four major classes：
1. Flammable（可燃）solid is one that can ignite from friction or from heat remaining from
its manufacture.
2. Flammable liquid is one having a flash point below 60.5°C.
3. A combustible（易燃） liquid has a flash point in excess of 60.5 °C, but below 93.3°C.
4. A flammable compressed gas meets specified criteria for lower flammability limit （可燃
性下限，LFL）， flammability range, and flame projection.
11
SUSTechS

## Page 12

19.4 Flammable and Combustible Substances
• Lower flammability limit （LFL）： Values of the vaporlair ratio below which ignition
cannot occur, because of insufficient fuel define the LFL.
• Upper flammability limit （UFL）： Values of the vapor/air ratio above which ignition
cannot occur because of insufficient air.
• Flammability range: The difference between UFL and LFL at a specified temperature.

北京交通大学实验室致3死爆炸事故调查报告公布
2019-02-13 20:16:04
来源：北京日报客户端
原标题：北京交通大学"12-26"事故调查报告公布
2018年12月26日，北京交通大学市政与环境工程实验室发生爆炸燃烧，事故造成3
人死亡。事故发生后，市委主要领导作出指示，要求全力开展抢险救援，确保现场安全
处置；市政府主要领导迅速赶赴事故现场，指挥部署抢险救援和应急处置。市政府随即
成立事故调查组，市纪委市监委派员参加调查处理。
事故调查组按照“科学严谨、依法依规、实事求是、注重实效”的原则，通过现场勘
验、检测鉴定、调查取证、模拟实验，并委托化工、爆炸、刑侦、火灾调查有关领域专
家组成专家组进行深入分析和反复论证，查明了事故发生的经过和原因，认定了事故性
质和责任，并提出了对有关责任人员和单位的处理建议及事故防范和整改措施。近日，
市政府正式批复了北京交通大学“12-26”事故调查报告。经事故调查组认定，本起事故是
一起责任事故。
经查，该起事故直接原因为：在使用搅拌机对镁粉和磷酸搅拌、反应过程中，料斗
内产生的氢气被搅拌机转轴处金属摩擦、碰撞产生的火花点燃爆炸，继而引发镁粉粉生
云爆炸，爆炸引起周边镁粉和其他可燃物燃烧，造成现场3名学生烧死。
炫图
石家庄退
河北邯郸：舞龙

Spontaneous Ignition
Pyrophoric substances（自燃物质）：Substances that catch fire spontaneously in air
without an ignition source.
Typical pyrophoric substances：
• White phosphorus
• Alkali metals
• Finely divided metals, such as Mg, Ca, Co, Mn, Fe, Zr, AI
• Organometallic compounds, such as ethyllithium （LiC2Hs）， phenyllithium （LiCeHs）
• Metal hydrides, such as LiH, B,Hg, AsHs

## Page 22

19.12 Hazardous Wastes and the Anthrosphere
Movement of hazardous wastes through the anthrosphere governed by a Manifest System
The goals of the Manifest System（清单系统）
• Acts as a tracking device to establish responsibility for the generation, movement，
treatment, and disposal of the waste.
•By requiring the manifest to accompany the waste, such as during truck transport,it
provides information regarding appropriate actions to take during emergencies such as
collisions, spills, fires, or explosions.
• Acts as the basic documentation for record keeping and reporting.
22

Major consideration is movement of wastes carried by water
• Plume of contaminated water
• Leachate from waste spilled on geosphere or placed in landfills
• Attenuation of wastes by geosphere solids
Quantify of Attenuation can be described by the Distribution coefficient, Kd
Ka=Cw
Cs, Cw are equilibrium concentrations of the constituent on solids and in solution.

19.16 Hazardous Wastes in the Biosphere
Bioaccumulation of substances in tissue
1. Biodegradation to simpler compounds
• Mineralization completely to simple inorganics （CO2,NH +）
2. Detoxification to less toxic materials
3. Microbial metabolism in waste degradation
• Oxic （aerobic） processes with Oz oxidant
• Anoxic （anaerobic） processes in absence of Oz
• Cometabolism （xenobiotic）
Ecotoxicology of hazardous wastes
• Ecotoxicology is the study of how chemicals affect organisms in the environment
• Important aspect in risk assessment of hazardous wastes

20.11 Preparation of Wastes for Disposal
Immobilization固定化
• Minimize exposed surface area to reduce leaching
•Physical and chemical processes
Stabilization稳定化
• Conversion to a physically and chemically more stable form
• Less likely to cause problems
• Less likely to be mobile after disposal
Solidification固化 of liquid or semisolid wastes to solid form
• Example: Binding with portland cement
•Example: Sorption onto a solid activated carbon matrix
• Example: Thermoplastics to bind wastes to a solid form
• Example: Vitrification or glassification
• Example:Solidification with Cement
Chemical Fixation化学固定
Binds a hazardous waste substance in a less mobile, toxic form by chemical reaction？

## Page 28

20.12 Ultimate Disposal of Wastes
Aboveground disposal地上处置
• May avoid some leaching problems common to subsurface disposal
Landfill填埋
• Most common disposal method
• In excavations lined with impermeable polymer and clay
•Capping required
• Cover waste • Prevent water infiltration
• Prevent release to atmosphere
• Leachate is a major problem
Surface impoundment in a pit液体的地表储存
• May be filled to produce a landfill after water evaporates
Deep well disposal of liquids深井处置液体
• Groundwater pollution is a major concern

## Page 29

20.14 In-Situ Waste Treatment
Wastes sequestered in place
• Precipitate heavy metals as sulfides
•Coprecipitate heavy metals with iron（III） or Mn（IV） oxides
Vapor extraction to pump vapors from waste sites
• Vapors may be burned in engines
Solidification in-situ, such as with silicates
Detoxification in-situ
• Chemical agents such as base to neutralize waste acid
• Biological detoxification of hazardous wastes difficult due to toxic conditions in waste
sites
In-situthermal processes
Soil Washing and Flushing
• Soil flushing when soil is left in place
• Soil washing when soil is removed, washed, replaced

Toxicology is the science of poisons

• Site of exposure
• Form of toxicant

## Page 31

Chapter 22: Toxicological Chemistry
Four major types of exposure
•Acute Local Exposure（急性局部暴露）
Exposure occurs at a specific location over a time period of a few seconds.
• Chronic Local Exposure（慢性局部暴露）
Generally low prolonged or repeated exposure
• Acute Systemic Exposure（急性全身暴露）
Systemic spread throughout organism
• Chronic Systematic Exposure（慢性全身暴露）
The exposure occurs over a prolonged time period

Synergism（协同），Potentiation（增效），Antagonism（颉颃）of Toxicants
Chemical interactions between toxicants affect toxicities
• Synergistic effects when the overall effect of two substances is greater than the sum
of their parts
• Potentiation when a substance inactive by itself enhances the action of an active
substance
• Antagonism when an active substance decreases the effect of another active one

## Page 45

3. Carcinogenesis（致癌）
Cancer is the uncontrolled replication and growth of the body's own cells
Carcinogenic agents are primarily
• Chemical agents, such as nitrosamines（亚胺）and polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons
• Biological agents, such as hepadna viruses（嗜肝DNA病毒）
• lonizing radiation, such as X-rays
• Genetic factors, such as selective breeding

Heavy Metals
• Beryllium: Light metal, cause of potentially fatal lung fibrosis（纤维化）and

•Cadmium: Highly toxic heavy metal, affects enzymes, cause of pneumonitis（肺炎）
• Lead: Of particular concern because of widespread distribution, prevents hemoglobin
synthesis, adversely affects nervous system
• Arsenic: Metalloid（非金属），coagulates proteins, interferes with metabolic processes
• Mercury: Liquid metal, highly toxic as metal vapor, inorganic salts, organometallic
compounds, affects central nervous system

## Page 1

1
Lecture 18: Sustainable Energy: The Key to Everything
/f we have sufficient sustainable energy...
• Water: Wastewater and saline water can be converted to water pure enough to drink
• Food: Marginal land can be reclaimed, water can be pumped long distances，
greenhouses for growing vegetables can be heated
• Wastes: All hazardous wastes converted to benign forms
• Transportation: Electrified railways and vehicles can be used for most land
transportation
•Fuels: Biomass sources of fixed carbon can be converted to hydrocarbon fuels to be
used in applications for which there are no viable alternatives （such as aircraft） without
emitting net amounts of carbon dioxide
€ SUSTechE

## Page 2

18.2. Nature of Energy
Energy is the capacity to do work （move matter）
Heat is energy in the movement of atoms and molecules
Kinetic energy is contained in moving objects
• Flywheels used for energy storage
Potential energy is stored energy
• Water in a reservoir that can be used in turbines
•Chemical energy released in chemical reactions
CH +20 CO+2H0
• Electrical energy in charged capacitors
Mechanical energy in a spinning turbine and generator armature
Electrical energy converted from mechanical energy in a generator

## Page 3

Units of Energy and Power
Thejoule （u） is the standard unit of energy
• 4.184 J raises temperature of 1g of liquid water 1°C
4.184 J = 1 calorie （cal）
Energy often expressed in kilojoule
• 1 kJ = 1000 J
Food energy commonly stated in units of 1000 cal, commonly called "calories”
• 1 food calorie = 1000 cal
Power
Power is energy per unit time
Watt = 1 joule per second （J.S-1）
•A typical compact fluorescent light bulb is 21 watts
• A large electrical power plant is typically 1000 megawatts （mw）
• Very large amounts of power in gigawatts （1 gw = 10° w）

## Page 9

Thermodynamics
Thermodynamics deals with energy in its various forms and with work
First law of thermodynamics states that energy is neither created nor destroyed
• Law of conservation of energy
The first law of thermodynamics is important in green science and technology the best
practice of which requires the most efficient use of energy
• According to thermodynamic laws only a fraction of chemical energy can be
converted to heat energy and then mechanical energy （as occurs in an internal
combustion engine）
• Typically, the "unused" energy is dissipated as heat
• Green technology utilizes this energy for heating

Listed wastes denoted by a letter and number

## Page 7

19.4 Flammable and Combustible Substances
• Flash pointis temperature of ignition in proximity to a flame under specified conditions
Four major classes：
1. Flammable solid is one that can ignite from friction or from heat remaining from its
manufacture.
2. Flammable liquid is one having a flash point below 60.5°C.
3. A combustible liquid has a flash point in excess of 60.5 °C, but below 93.3°C.
4. A flammable compressed gas meets specified criteria for lower flammability limit （LFL），
flammability range, and flame projection.
• Lower flammability limit （LFL） ： Values of the vaporlair ratio below which ignition cannot
occur, because of insufficient fuel define the LFL.
• Upper flammability limit （UFL）： Values of the vapor/air ratio above which ignition cannot
occur because of insufficient air
• Flammability range: The difference between UFL and LFL at a specified temperature

北京交通大学实验室致3死爆炸事故调查报告公布
2019-02-13 20:16:04
来源：北京日报客户端
原标题：北京交通大学"12-26"事故调查报告公布
2018年12月26日，北京交通大学市政与环境工程实验室发生爆炸燃烧，事故造成3
人死亡。事故发生后，市委主要领导作出指示，要求全力开展抢险救援，确保现场安全
处置；市政府主要领导迅速赶赴事故现场，指挥部署抢险救援和应急处置。市政府随即
成立事故调查组，市纪委市监委派员参加调查处理。
事故调查组按照“科学严谨、依法依规、实事求是、注重实效”的原则，通过现场勘
验、检测鉴定、调查取证、模拟实验，并委托化工、爆炸、刑侦、火灾调查有关领域专
家组成专家组进行深入分析和反复论证，查明了事故发生的经过和原因，认定了事故性
质和责任，并提出了对有关责任人员和单位的处理建议及事故防范和整改措施。近日，
市政府正式批复了北京交通大学“12-26”事故调查报告。经事故调查组认定，本起事故是
一起责任事故。
经查，该起事故直接原因为：在使用搅拌机对镁粉和磷酸搅拌、反应过程中，料斗
内产生的氢气被搅拌机转轴处金属摩擦、碰撞产生的火花点燃爆炸，继而引发美美粉粉尘
云爆炸，爆炸引起周边镁粉和其他可燃物燃烧，造成现场3名学生烧死。
炫图
石家庄退
河北邯郸：舞龙

Spontaneous Ignition
Pyrophoric substances: Substances that catch fire spontaneously in air without an
ignition source.
Typical pyrophoric substances：
•White phosphorus
• Alkali metals
• Finely divided metals, such as Mg, Ca, Co, Mn, Fe, Zr, AI
•Organometallic compounds, such as ethyllithium （LiCzHs）， phenyllithium （LiCeHs）
• Metal hydrides, such as LiH, B,Hg, AsHs
Moisture in air is often a factor in spontaneous ignition.

## Page 21

19.11 Transport, Effects, and Fates of Hazardous Wastes
Transport of hazardous wastes depends on
• Physical properties of the substances, including vapor pressure and solubility；
• Physical properties of the surrounding matrix；
• Physical conditions to which wastes are subjected. Higher temperatures and erosive
wind conditions enable volatile substances to move more readily；
•Chemical and biochemical properties of wastes. Substances that are less chemically
reactive and less biodegradable will tend to move farther before breaking down.

•Air •Water • Soil
Fates of Hazardous Wastes
In waterlargely determined by substance's

•Biodegradability•Chemical reactivity
In the atmosphere determined largely by
• Volatility • Solubility （in rainfall）

19.12 Hazardous Wastes and the Anthrosphere
Hazardous wastes originate in the anthrosphere in different physical forms
• Liquid•semisolid sludges•solids
Hazardous wastes release from the anthrosphere through
• Liquid spills•Accidental discharges • Fires
• Explosions
To minimize the hazardous waste generators are required to have
• Specified equipment•Trained personnel
• Procedures that in event of release
• Protect human health•Facilitate remediation
Also required are
• Means of communication • Firefighting capabilities
• Neutralizing agents•Adsorbents

## Page 24

19.12 Hazardous Wastes and the Anthrosphere
Hazardous materials move, have effects, and end up in the anthrosphere as well.
Movement of hazardous wastes through the anthrosphere governed by a Manifest System
The goals of the Manifest System
• Acts as a tracking device to establish responsibility for the generation, movement，
treatment, and disposal of the waste.
• By requiring the manifest to accompany the waste, such as during truck transport, it
provides information regarding appropriate actions to take during emergencies such as
collisions, Spills, fires,or explosions.
• Acts as the basic documentation for record keeping and reporting.

19.17 Hazardous Substances in Terrorism
Hazardous substances that could be used in terrorist attacks include
1. Toxic and otherwise hazardous industrial chemicals
2. Military poisons
3. Combustible substances and oxidizers
4. Corrosive substances
5. Highly reactive substances including explosives
Other kinds of substances potentially used in terrorist attacks
•Oxidizers （NH,NOs）•Corrosive substances （H,S0A）
•Explosives
